5.3.
Properties of Well-formed Programs

We highlight the properties satisfied by well-formed programs (proofs in the Appendix):

•

Progress and Preservation (
Theorems

5.2
and
5.1
). This
means that if a well-formed program is not finished (empty state), is not an
error (normal, absent, permission, or cast error), or is not in a deadlock state
(terminal configuration), then it can take a reduction step until it ends in a
terminal configuration state and the result of each reduction step is well-formed.

•

Data-Race Freedom (
Theorem

5.5
). Programs without unsafe objects are
data-race free by construction.

•

Dynamic Gradual Guarantee (
Theorem

5.6
). Adaptation of the
gradual guarantee
(
Siek and Taha 2006
;
Siek
et al. 2015
)
stating
capabilities do not affect the run-time semantics, modulo casts and capability
errors. Essentially, if an unsafe program is well-formed and takes a reduction
step, the same program with capability annotations either reduces to the same
run-time configuration (modulo safe erasure,
Appendix

B

Definition

B.11
)
or throws an error due to a cast or capability violation.

Programs start in an initial well-formed configuration
\epsilon;t
(Appendix,
Definition

B.1
),
and reduce to new configurations. Progress (
Theorem

5.1
) guarantees that
a well-formed configuration reduces to a new configuration, or it is terminal (Appendix,
Definition

B.2
).
From Preservation (
Theorem

5.2
), a reduction step always leads to
a well-formed configuration. Terminal configurations are either finished programs,
errors (with
\overline{T}\ \textit{Err}\equiv\textit{Err}
from equivalence rules on Page
5.1
), or a deadlock configuration (Appendix,
Definition

B.3
).
A deadlock configuration happens when all threads are either waiting on a receive
or on a send operation.

Theorem 5.1 (Progress).

A well-formed configuration
\Gamma\vdash H;\overline{T}
is either
a terminal configuration or

H;\overline{T}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}
.

Theorem 5.2 (Preservation).

If
\Gamma\vdash H;t\ \overline{T}
is a well-formed configuration, and
H;t\ \overline{T}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T}
then, there exists a

\Gamma^{\prime}
s.t.
\Gamma^{\prime}\supseteq\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T}

Corollary 5.3 (Thread-Affinity of Thread-Local Fields).

Implied by Preservation, a thread local object with owner
i
cannot contain a thread local
object with owner
j
, where
i\neq j
. The only way a local object can reference another local object
of a different owner is via field assignment (
R-FieldAssign
). But
R-FieldAssign

checks that target and source share owners. Thus, thread local objects can only reference
thread local objects of the same owner.

Definition 5.4 (Data Race).

Informally, a data race is defined as two threads accessing
(write-write or read-write) the same field without any
interleaving synchronisation. In our setting, this translates to
two accesses to the same field of an object
o
in threads
i

and
j
without an interleaving explicit transfer of
o
from

i
to
j
. Thus, a data race in Dalarna requires the ability of a mutable object to be
referenced from two threads at the same time (formal definition in
Definition

B.4
).

Theorem

5.5
states that Dalarna is data race-free modulo unsafe objects.
Objects that use safe capabilities cannot introduce a data race; unsafe objects
may introduce data races.

Theorem 5.5 (Dalarna is Data-Race Free Modulo Unsafe Objects).

All data races directly or indirectly involve an
unsafe object. A data race is defined as a read/write or
write/write access to an object by different threads without
interleaving synchronisation (In our formalism, this
means an interleaving transfer of an object to another thread; formal proof in Appendix).

We now proceed to sketch the proof of data-race freedom for safe
objects by showing that it is not possible for a mutable safe
object to be aliased from two different threads at the same time.
We refer to
l
,
i
,
j
,
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
from
Definition

5.4
for
clarity.

Let us examine the implications of
l
having any of the three
safe capabilities (and ignore unsafe objects for now).

(1)

l
is immutable
. By
E-BadFieldAssign
, attempts to
write fields of an immutable object will err. Thus, if
l
is
immutable, then
R
will contain an error, which it did not by
assumption.

(2)

l
is local
. By
R-Field
and
R-FieldAssign
,
attempts to write a field of a local from outside of its
creating thread will err. Thus, if
l
is local, then
R
will
contain an error (because
i\neq j
), which it did not by
assumption.

(3)

l
is isolated
. Fields of isolated objects can be
read or written freely. Thus, we must show that
l
can be
accessible in two threads at the same time. In the initial heap,
no objects exist that is shared across threads and our only way
to share objects across threads is by sending them on a channel.
An isolated
l
can be sent on a channel. However, this requires
that
l
is consumed, meaning it will no longer be accessible by
the sender. To avoid the consumption, we could store
l
in a
field of an object, and then transfer the object. Such objects,
would have to be immutable or local. However, by

R-FieldAssign
, immutable or local objects cannot contain
isolated objects.

Thus, we cannot create a situation where
l
is in both
r_{1}

and
r_{2}
without any interleaving send.

A program that uses unsafe objects may use these to store local
and isolated objects. Thus, an unsafe object
u
aliased across
threads could store a local or isolated
l
in
u.f
(see 3. above). This would
allow threads
i
and
j
to do
u.f.g
. If
l
is local,
unless
i=j
, at least one of the accesses will err (or both if
neither
i
nor
j
is the creating thread of
l
, see 2. above). If
l
is
isolated,
u.f.g
will err by
E-IsoField
. Thus, even in the
presence of unsafe objects, safe objects will not participate in
data races.

Progress and preservation guarantee the absence of untrapped errors and
Theorem

5.5

shows that all data races can be blamed on unsafe objects. Next, we show that capability
annotations do not affect the run-time semantics, modulo cast or capability
violations which trap operations that if allowed could lead to a data race.
We show this in the Dynamic Gradual Guarantee theorem (
Theorem

5.6
, adapted from
(
Siek
et al. 2015
)
).
We unpack this theorem before stating it formally. Let
P
be a
well-formed program, and
S
its “stripped equivalent”, where
all safe capabilities have been erased (and thus replaced with unsafe).
Below, “reduces” denotes a single reduction step.

(1)

The well-formedness of
S
follows from the well-formedness
of
P
, as an unsafe object can reference any object
(
Appendix

B

Definition

B.11
).

(2)

If
P
reduces to a non-error configuration,
S
reduces to
the same configuration and the same heap (modulo heap capability
erasure); in case
P
throws an error caused by absent values
or normal errors,
S
will throw the same error. The two
evaluations only diverge if
P
throws a permission error in a
dynamic check – this will never happen in
S
because it
does not have any safe capabilities. (See
Lemma

B.27

e.g.,

E-BadFieldAssign
or
E-BadInstantiation

among others.)

(3)

If
S
reduces to an error configuration,
P
will reduce to
the same error configuration.
If
S
reduces to a non-error
configuration,
P
will either reduce to the same
configuration (modulo capability stripping), or throw a
capability or permission error.

Theorem 5.6 (Dynamic Gradual Guarantee).

Let
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}
be a configuration and
\Gamma
a store type such that
\Gamma\vdash H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}
.
Let
e
be a function that replaces safe capabilities with unsafe in heaps, terms, etc (
Definition

B.11
). Then:

(1)

\Gamma^{e}\vdash(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
.

(2)

(a)

If
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T_{1}}\ \overline{T_{0}}

and
\overline{T_{1}}\neq\textit{Err}
then,

(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}\rightsquigarrow(H^{\prime};\overline{T_{1}}\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
.

(b)

If
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T_{1}}\ \overline{T_{0}}
and

\overline{T_{1}}=\textmd{Err}_{A}\lor\textmd{Err}_{N}
then,

(H;t\ \overline{T})^{e}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime\prime};\overline{T_{1}}\ \overline{T_{0}}

(3)

(a)

If
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}\rightsquigarrow(H^{\prime};\textit{Err}\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}

then,
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\textit{Err}\ \overline{T_{0}}

(b)

If
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}\rightsquigarrow(H^{\prime};\overline{T}\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}

and
\overline{T}\neq\textit{Err}
then,

H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T_{0}}
and

\overline{T^{\prime}}=\textmd{Err}_{P}\lor\textmd{Err}_{C}\lor\overline{T}
.

The Dynamic Gradual Guarantee (
Theorem

5.6
) uses a single step
reduction to guarantee that the capabilities are semantics preserving, modulo
permission and cast errors. We extend the Dynamic Gradual Guarantee to account
for multi-step reductions, starting from an initial configuration until reaching a
terminal configuration,
i.e.,

\epsilon;P\rightsquigarrow^{*}H;C
. To remove
non-determinism of program reductions, we define the trace of a program as a list
of pairs that contain the reduction step and the thread id on which the reduction happens.
We extend the reduction relation to account for the trace,
named the replay reduction relation, which is the standard reduction relation
except that it deterministically applies the expected reduction step on the
expected thread id (Appendix,

Definitions

B.5
,
B.6
and
B.7
and
Theorem

B.28
).
The basic idea is to reduce a safe program to a terminal configuration,
which produces a trace. We use this trace to replay the reductions on the capability
stripped (unsafe) program (and
vice versa
), showing two programs reduce
to the same terminal configuration modulo cast errors and permission errors.
Since it obscures some cases where the identical step is taken, we show the single-step
theorem in the paper which highlights these cases.

6.
Daddala: A Prototype Implementation of Dala in Grace

To be able to explore the applicability of our capability model, we have embedded it within a
preexisting general-purpose object-oriented language, Grace
(
Black
et al. 2012
)
. We
extended an existing implementation to support run-time enforcement of
capabilities with the correct dynamic semantics. Programs in the formal syntax
have a straightforward translation into the Grace syntax, and the wider features
of the language are also usable with minimal limitations.

Our implementation, Daddala
11
11

11

Daddala
is a genus of moth in the family Erebidæ.
, is fully embedded within the existing Grace
syntax, with no syntactic changes to the host language.
The embedded syntax will be familiar to the formal syntax above.

Objects in Grace are created with object literals

object

\{

...

\}
, which instantiate new anonymous
objects with the given methods, fields, and inline initialisation code. To
specify the capability of the object we use the existing inheritance
system
(
Noble
et al. 2017
;
Jones
et al. 2016
)
and write

object

\{
use

isolate
\}
(or immutable, local; unsafe
is the default).

let

(
e
)

in

\{

x

\to

t

\}
,

spawn

\{

x

\to

t

\}
, and

consume
(
x
)

perform the corresponding roles.
Send and receive is
c

\leftarrow

5
and
\leftarrow

c
.
Casting is implemented straightforwardly as Grace
type annotations precede the capability name:
var

x

:

iso

:=

y
.

6.1.
Extending Grace with Dala Capabilities

Our prototype extends Grace in two ways:
every assignment performs some check and
objects are tagged at creation with their capabilities. Objects are tagged unsafe
(default), immutable, local, free isolate, or bound isolate.

An isolate is
free
if it is not currently stored in a variable
or field, otherwise
bound
. These are the only two capability tags that are not fixed for the
object’s lifetime (distinguishing this system from Grace’s
brands
(
Jones
et al. 2015
)
, which are immutable). When a variable
or field containing a (bound) isolate is assigned to, the
assignment re-tags the object as a free isolate. When

any
storage location is assigned to, the assignment
checks the right-hand side and raises an exception if it is
a bound isolate (as this would otherwise violate alias-freedom),
or re-tags a free isolate as a bound isolate
before storing it. In essence, this pair behaves as a one-bit
reference count for isolate objects.

Assignments occur at assignment statements (
x

:=

y
), variable and field
declarations (
var

x

:=

y
,
def

x

=

y
), binding of method parameters
(
method

foo
(
x
)

\{\};

foo
(
y
)
), and binding of block (first-class
lambda) parameters (
\{

x

\to

\}.
apply
(
y
)
). All of these
locations perform the free/bound isolate handling above, while fields also
perform the necessary structural checks based on the capability of their
enclosing object and of the assigned object. These are the only places new kinds
of error have been introduced.

There is one special extension motivated by simplicity: the
consume
(
x
)

operation is syntactically rewritten to
(
x

:=

null
)
, rather
than truly a method as the syntax suggests.

6.2.
Limitations Due to Embedding

Some features of the Grace language run counter to the properties
of the formal model. While a program written according to the
model will execute correctly, it is possible to construct
pathological programs using other language features that violate
the imposed constraints in the present implementation. In
particular, Grace makes heavy use of lexical capture: all scopes
are closures, including object bodies
(
Bruce et al. 2013
)
. A
program such as the one in
Fig.

11
will access the
isolate object that should no longer be reachable, because the

y
object retains the lexical scope of the surrounding object
and can implicitly call its method
f
. Our prototype will not
raise an error at this point. This type of capture is unavoidable
without breaking fundamental aspects of the language (which uses
scoping to provide even builtins like
if-then-else
(
Homer
et al. 2014
)
), but the
use
of
improperly-captured objects can be detected statically. While it
is therefore possible to eliminate these errors (and other
implementation strategies, such as proxies for isolates, can
similarly address the issue), our current prototype permits a
programmer who goes far outside the model to shoot themselves in
the foot.

⬇

var

x

:=

object

{

use

isolate

method

f

{

}

method

g

{

object

{

method

h

{

f

}

}

}

}

def

y

=

x
.
g

def

c

=

spawn
{
v
\to
(
\leftarrow
v
)}

c

\leftarrow

consume
(
x
)

y
.
h

Figure 11.
Limitations due to embedding: current unsoundness of our implementation. See
Section

6.2
.

6.3.
Concluding Remarks

Extending an existing system to support capabilities in Dala style is
relatively straightforward; it is only the various kinds of variable assignment
that require meaningful intervention, plus a small object tag. We modified an
implementation that was previously designed with no concept of capabilities, so
that it now enforces them dynamically and supports a direct translation from the
formal model.

7.
Related Work

The Dala capability model (immutables
<
isolates
<
thread-locals)
carefully selects a number of well-known concepts from the literature

(
Lea 1999
;
Hogg 1991
;
Clarke et al. 2008
;
Wrigstad et al. 2009
;
Miller 2006b
;
Clebsch et al. 2015
)
. Dala’s key contribution here is the careful combination: what we have
left out is at least as important as the features we have included. Given that
an actor is essentially a thread plus thread-local storage
(
De Wael et al. 2015
)
,
our distinctions are similar to many object-actor hybrid systems

(
Schäfer and Poetzsch-Heffter 2010
;
Dedecker et al. 2006b
;
Cutsem et al. 2014
;
Gruber and Boyer 2013
;
Koster
et al. 2015
;
Koster
et al. 2016
)
,
although, crucially, we follow Singularity
(
Larus and Hunt 2010
)
by incorporating
isolates for fast transfers. Similarly, there are many more flexible models for
distinguishing between read-only and read-write objects: we adopt “deep
immutability” for its clear conceptual model Glacier
(
Coblenz et al. 2017
)
. While
there are certainly more complicated models of permissions and capabilities for
data-race freedom (
e.g.,

(
Clarke et al. 2008
;
Castegren and
Wrigstad 2016
;
Clebsch et al. 2015
;
Zhao and Boyland 2008
)
and many others), we consider our chosen set of
concepts a “sweet spot” in the balance between expressivity and complexity.

7.1.
Capabilities and Ownership

Dala is also heavily influenced by static capability-based programming
languages
(
Brandauer et al. 2015
;
Castegren and
Wrigstad 2016
;
Clebsch and
Drossopoulou 2013
;
Clebsch et al. 2015
;
Gordon et al. 2012
)
. Capability-based
programming languages require all programs to be fully annotated with
capabilities, and these annotations guarantee data-race freedom, with erasure
semantics. In contrast, our approach begins with a dynamic language
that allows data race and data race-free programs to interact, and we
maintain data race-freedom for programs with safe capabilities.

Sergey and Clarke
(
Sergey and Clarke 2012
)
add gradual ownership to a
static language, introducing notions of parametric ownership and inserting
casts when needed;
they prove soundness and common
ownership invariants.
Our work has similarities in that isolated objects can be seen as
owners-as-dominators, and our local objects have threads as owners. Dala
differs in that it is a dynamic, concurrent language, and we prove common invariants and
data race-freedom for safe objects.

HJp
(
Westbrook et al. 2012
)
enforces safe sharing of objects using a
permission-based gradual typing, which inserts run-time checks when necessary.
Objects are either in shared-read permission, which allows reading from multiple
threads but no mutation, or read-write permission, allows any mutation and
aliasing but no sharing. It also introduces storable permissions which allows a
permission to refer to a tree of objects. Our approach uses capabilities at
run-time;
imm
capabilities are similar to HJp
shared-read and read-write permissions are similar to
local
. In addition,
Dala also has the concept of
iso
which can move across threads
but do not allow any aliasing.

Roberts
et al.

(
Roberts
et al. 2019
)
(and recently
(
Moy et al. 2020
)
) showed that run-time gradual type checks could have
minimal or no performance impact on a suitable virtual machine, despite what is
naively much extra checking for partially-typed programs. We chose to extend
their Moth virtual machine for our dynamically-checked implementation to take
advantage of their work.

7.2.
Capabilities In The Wild

Castegren’s
et al
work
(
Castegren and
Wrigstad 2016
)
seems
to be the first one where reference capabilities are orthogonal to the
concurrency model
i.e.,
the reference capabilities seem applicable to multiple concurrency models.
Their capabilities have been formalised using fork-join style
but their implementation uses active objects
(
Brandauer et al. 2015
)
.
The implementation of Gordon’s
et al
reference immutability work closely
follows the formal semantics; because their reference permissions apply
transitively to fields of the object, it is not clear whether the model is
general enough to be applicable to different concurrency models,
e.g.,
actor or tuplespace
model
(
de Boer et al. 2017
;
Gelernter 1985
)
.
Boyland et al’s work
(
Boyland
et al. 2001
)
can encode 8
object capabilities to express different invariants and they argue that these can
be used in concurrent programs with no run-time cost, when programs are fully
typed. We believe the capability system is expressive enough to work
on different concurrency models, but it is not clear whether their capabilities enforce data
race freedom.
In languages such as E
(
Miller 2006a
)
, AmbientTalk
(
Cutsem et al. 2014
)
, and
Newspeak
(
Bracha 2017
)
references (far and near) represent object
capabilities
(
Dennis and Horn 1966
)
and use a
vat
-based concurrency model.
In contrast, our work is simple and uses 3 capabilities, allows
interoperation between safe and “racy” programs and (as far as we know) it is the
first one to use reference capabilities in a dynamic language where the
capabilities are orthogonal to the concurrency model.

7.3.
Race Detectors

Although our capability checks guarantee data-race freedom,
they
are different to the checks that a data-race detector might employ
(
Savage et al. 1997
)
.
These checks are also in some sense “eager” or may cause false
positives. For example, a program that effectively transfers a
mutable object
o
between two threads will execute without errors
if
o
is isolated, but not if it is local and the non-owning thread dereferences
it. This is a somewhat pragmatic choice, but
guided by our desire to make our capabilities a tool for
programmers to capture their
intent
. Thus, we expect that a
local object is explicitly demarcated local (at creation time) and
not isolated for a reason. Thus, Dala helps programmers state
their intentions and check that the programs they write conform to
said intentions. This is different from a data race detector which
may only fire if a data race occurs (which may happen on
some runs but not others of the same program).

8.
Discussion and Future Work

Our claim of efficiency rests on absence of deep copying and turning
local accesses into asynchronous operations. That said, our capabilities
incur a cost on (most) accesses to objects –
e.g.,
on writes to
fields, etc. To remove most of this cost will require
self-optimising run-times
(
Wimmer and
Würthinger 2012
)
and techniques similar to
Grace’s transient checks
(
Roberts
et al. 2019
)
to reduce the
number of checks needed to satisfy the capability invariants.

Adding gradual capabilities at the type level will allow most
checks to be removed
(
Roberts
et al. 2019
;
Moy et al. 2020
)
, but more importantly help programmers
document the behaviour of code. Notably this addition will not need
escape hatches due to inflexible types as programmers can fall-back to
dynamic checks which are equally safe.

In this paper, the Dala capabilities only constrain heap
structures. Nothing prevents a stack variable in an immutable
object to point to an unsafe object. To reason about data-race
freedom of a method call on a safe object, we need to consider the
methods arguments’ capabilities. Extending the structural
constraints to stack variables is an interesting point in the
design space: if immutable objects can only “see” other
immutable objects, method calls on immutables are guaranteed to be
side-effect free modulo allocation and GC. For isos, side-effects
are not possible, but preexisting objects may be updated in place.
Finally, local objects would only observe local objects belonging
to the same thread (which is probably very desirable), and permit
side-effects visible in the current thread only. Such a design can
reduce the number of checks (
e.g.,
all checks of thread-ownership
happen only when calling a local method in an unsafe context).

9.
Conclusion

A data race is a fundamental, low-level aspect of a program which
is not tied to the intended semantics of a particular application.
While many race conditions stem from data-races, data-race freedom
does not mean absence of race conditions. Data-race freedom is
however still important: removing them removes many race
conditions and moreover makes a program’s semantics independent on
the idiosyncrasies of a particular (weak) memory model. In the
case of programming languages like C and C++, data-races are
examples undefined behaviour. The Dala capabilities guarantee
absence of data races in safe objects by imposing restrictions on
all code that interacts with these objects. Safe and unsafe objects
can co-exist and the presence of the latter does not compromise
the guarantees of the former.

Dala helps programmers structure their programs with capabilities
including immutable, isolated, and thread-local. We support the Dala design with a formal
model, clear and proven properties
w.r.t.
data-race freedom and
semantics preservation when capabilities are added to a program.

We provide Daddala (
Section

6
), an early proof-of-concept prototype implementation
which is available as open source.
12
12

12

https://github.com/gracelang/moth-SOMns/tree/daddala

Based on this last experience, we believe that our model can
provide opt-in data-race safety to programmers on top of existing
languages, with relatively little implementation difficulty and
overhead.

Appendix B
Definition, Lemmas, and Proofs

Definition B.1 (Initial Configuration).

An initial configuration is a closed term with empty heap,
\epsilon;t
.

Definition B.2 (Terminal Configuration).

A well-formed configuration
\Gamma\vdash H;\overline{T}

is terminal if
it contains zero threads
(
\overline{T}=\epsilon
),
it is an error
(
\overline{T}=\textit{Err}
),
or if it is a deadlock configuration
(
\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;\overline{T})
).

Definition B.3 (Deadlock Configuration).

A deadlocked configuration is a well-formed configuration
where all threads are blocked on sends and receives.

\displaystyle\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;\overline{T})\iff

{{{\displaystyle\overline{T}\neq\epsilon\land\forall T^{\prime}\in\overline{T}.\lor\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}T^{\prime}=E[\ \leftarrow\iota]\land H(\iota)=\,\!\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{chan}}}}}}}\,\{\_,\varnothing\}\\
T^{\prime}=E[x\leftarrow\_]\land H(\iota)=\,\!\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{chan}}}}}}}\,\{\_,v\}\\
T^{\prime}=E[\blacksquare_{i}\,\iota]\land H(\iota)=\,\!\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{chan}}}}}}}\,\{i,\_\}\end{array}\right.

Definition B.4 (Data Race).

To formally define a data race, we introduce the notion of a
trace
R
, which is an ordered sequence of reductions
r_{1}
,

r_{2}
… in the evaluation of a program
P
. We only concern
ourselves with traces that do not evaluate to a permission or cast error.

We define the precedes relation
<
on reductions thus: If

R=r_{1},R^{\prime}
, then
r_{1}<r_{2}
for all
r_{2}\in R^{\prime}
. Recall that
threads have unique ids.

We are now ready to formally define a data race in Dalarna.
A program has a data race if it may give rise to at least one
(non-erring) trace with a data race. A trace
R
has a data race if
it contains steps
r_{1}<r_{2}
such that

(1)

r_{1}=H_{1};E[x.f=\_]^{i}
, and
H_{1}(x)=l

(thread
i
writes
l.f
)

(2)

r_{2}=H_{2};E[y.f=\_]^{j}
or
r_{2}=H_{2};E[y.f]^{j}
, and
H_{2}(y)=l

(thread
j
reads or writes
l.f
)

(3)

i\neq j

(the conflicting accesses take place in different threads

and there does not exist
r_{3},r_{4}\in R
such that

(1)

r_{1}<r_{3}<r_{4}<r_{2}
,

(2)

r_{3}=H_{3};E[\_\leftarrow v_{1}]^{i}
and
l\in ROG(H,v_{1})

(thread
i
sends
l
somewhere)

(3)

r_{4}=H_{4};[\leftarrow v_{2}]^{j}
and
l\in ROG(H,v_{2})

(thread
j
receives
l
from somewhere)

where
ROG(H,v)
denotes the transitive closure of objects (the Reachable Object Graph) in
H
rooted in
v
.

Definition B.5 (Trace).

Define a trace
\mathcal{T}
as a list of pairs containing the reduction rules and thread ids.

Definition B.6 (Trace Of A Program).

Define the trace
\mathcal{T}
of a program
P
as the accumulation
of pairs of reduction rules and thread ids on which the reduction rules take place,

e.g.,

\epsilon;P\rightsquigarrow^{*}H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}
produces trace
\mathcal{T}
.

Definition B.7 (Trace Reduction Replay).

Define a trace replay reduction relation
(\mathcal{T:T^{\prime}});H;\overline{T}\rightsquigarrow{}\mathcal{T^{\prime}};H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}
as the reduction
relation that executes the first item of the trace
\mathcal{T:T^{\prime}}
at a time,
returning the trace list without the first item (
\mathcal{T}
), a new heap
(
H^{\prime}
), and a new thread configuration (
\overline{T^{\prime}}
).

Definition

B.8
states that if an object is reachable from threads
t

and
t^{\prime}
then either
t=t^{\prime}
or the object is not local or the object can only be
dereference in a single thread (via ownership check). Thus, thread-local objects
are not reachable by multiple threads. The helper function
\textsf{ROG}(H,t)

returns the set of objects in
H
reachable from
t
by traversing variables and
fields (a reflexive transitive closure of objects).

Definition B.8 (Thread-Local Objects).

A configuration satisfies object “thread-locality” if no object with a local capability is reachable from more than one thread.

\displaystyle\textsf{Local}(H,\overline{T})\iff\forall t^{i},t^{\prime j}\in\overline{T}.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\iota\in(\textsf{ROG}(H,t^{i})\cap\textsf{ROG}(H,t^{\prime j}))\Rightarrow

{{\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(t=t^{\prime}\land i=j)\lor(H(\iota)=K\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\_\,\_\}\land K\neq\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{local}}}}}}}{})\lor

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(t\neq t^{\prime}\land i\neq j\land(\textsf{isLocal}(H,\iota)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Rightarrow\textsf{isOwner}(H,i,\iota)\land\neg\textsf{isOwner}(H,j,\iota))

Object isolation (
Definition

B.9
) states that all isolated objects have a single reference to them, from the heap or stack,
modulo borrowing. Borrowing allows calling methods on isolated objects without consuming
the target, leading to (temporal) aliases which are all on the stack of the same thread.
We forbid consuming
self
, thus
aliases of isolated objects (and
self
) are only introduced through method calls. After a method
call, the alias is buried and non-accessible.

Definition B.9 (Object Isolation).

A configuration satisfies object isolation if no objects with an
iso
capability have more than one incoming pointer from the stack and heap, modulo borrowing.

\displaystyle\textsf{Isolated}(H,\overline{T})\iff

\displaystyle\quad\forall\iota\in\textsf{Isos}(H)\,.\,|\textsf{Inc}(H,\overline{T},\iota)\cup\textsf{Inc}(H,\iota)|>1\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\quad\exists t\in\overline{T}\,.\,\textsf{Inc}(H,\overline{T},\iota)=\textsf{Inc}(H,t,\iota)\land\textsf{Inc}(H,\iota)=\emptyset

where
Inc
collects the set of variables and fields aliasing a particular location:

{\textsf{Inc}(H,v)=\{\iota.f\,|\,\iota\in\textit{dom}(H)\land H(\iota.f)=v\}\cup\{\iota\,|\,\iota\in\textit{dom}(H)\land H(\iota)=\,\!\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{chan}}}}}}}\,\{i,v\}\}
,

\textsf{Inc}(H,\overline{T},v)=\bigcup_{t\in\overline{T}}\textsf{Inc}(H,t,v)
,

\textsf{Inc}(H,t,v)=\{x\,|\,x\in\textsf{FreeVars}(t)\land H(x)=v\}\cup\{v\mid v\in t\}
, and
\textsf{FreeVars}(t)
is the set of all free variables in
t
.

Definition B.10 (Object’s Owner).

Define the predicate
\textsf{isOwner}(H,i,v)
for a heap (
H
), owner (
i
),
and value (
v
) as:

{\displaystyle\textsf{isOwner}(H,i,v)\iff H(\iota)=K^{i}\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\_\,\_\},

\displaystyle\qquad\textit{where}\ v=\iota\land\iota\in\textit{dom}(H)

Definition B.11 (Safe Erasure).

Define the function
safe erasure
,
e
, as the function that replaces
safe capabilities to
unsafe
capability as follows:

{\displaystyle(\Gamma,x:K)^{e}=(\Gamma)^{e},x:\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{unsafe}}}}}}}

{\displaystyle(\Gamma,\iota:K)^{e}=(\Gamma)^{e},\iota:\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{unsafe}}}}}}}

\displaystyle(H,x\mapsto\iota)^{e}=(H)^{e},x\mapsto\iota

{{\displaystyle(H,\iota\mapsto\,\!\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{chan}}}}}}}\,\{i,v\})^{e}=(H)^{e},\iota\mapsto\,\!\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{chan}}}}}}}\,\{i,v\}

{{{\displaystyle(H,\iota\mapsto K\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\_\,\_\})^{e}=(H)^{e},\iota\mapsto\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{unsafe}}}}}}}\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\_\,\_\}

\displaystyle(t\ \overline{T})^{e}=(t)^{e}\ (\overline{T})^{e}

{{{{{{{{{{{{{{{\displaystyle(t)^{e}=\begin{cases}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{unsafe}}}}}}}{}\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\overline{f=(v)^{e}}\,\overline{(M)^{e}}\},&t=K\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\overline{f=v}\,\overline{M}\}\\
(\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{unsafe}}}}}}})~\iota,&t=(K)~\iota\\
\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{unsafe}}}}}}}\ \text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{copy}}}}}}}\ x,&t=K\ \text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{copy}}}}}}}\ x\\
\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~(e)^{e}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~(t)^{e},&t=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t\\
\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{spawn}}}}}}}~(x)~\{(t)^{e}\},&t=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{spawn}}}}}}}~(x)~\{t\}\\
\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{method}}}}}}}~m(x)~\{(t)^{e}\},&t=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{method}}}}}}}~m(x)~\{t\}\\
t,&\textit{otherwise}\end{cases}

Definition B.12 (Capability Extraction).

{{{Cap(H,x)=\begin{cases}K,&H(x)=\iota\land H(\iota)=K\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\_\,\_\}\\
\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{local}}}}}}}{},&H(x)=\iota\land H(\iota)=\,\!\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{chan}}}}}}}\,\{\_,\_\}\\
K,&H(x)=\top,\textit{ for some }K\end{cases}

Definition B.13 (Thread’s Variables).

The variables of a thread are all variables used in the thread.

{{\displaystyle\textsf{Vars}(\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t)=\{x\}\cup\textsf{Vars}(e)\cup\textsf{Vars}(t)

{{\displaystyle\textsf{Vars}(x)=\textsf{Vars}(\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{consume}}}}}}}\ x)=\textsf{Vars}(x.f)=\textsf{Vars}(K\ \text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{copy}}}}}}}\ x)=\{x\}

\displaystyle\textsf{Vars}(x.f=e)=\textsf{Vars}(x.m(e))=\{x\}\cup\textsf{Vars}(e)

\displaystyle\textsf{Vars}(e\leftarrow e^{\prime})=\textsf{Vars}(e)\cup\textsf{Vars}(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\textsf{Vars}((K)~e)=\textsf{Vars}(\ \leftarrow e)=\textsf{Vars}(e)

{\displaystyle\textsf{Vars}(K\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\overline{f=w}\,\_\})=\textsf{Vars}(\overline{w})

{\displaystyle\textsf{Vars}(\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{spawn}}}}}}}~(x)~\{t\})=\textsf{Vars}(\_)=\epsilon

Definition B.14 (Reachable Object Graph).

Define the helper function
\textsf{ROG}(H,t)
and
\textsf{ROG}(H,\iota)
as
the function that returns the transitive closure of objects reachable from term

t
and location
\iota
.

\displaystyle\textsf{ROG}(H,t)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{x\in\textsf{DefVar}(H,t)}\textsf{ROG}(H,H(x))\cup\{\textsf{ROG}(H,\iota)\mid\iota\in t\}

\displaystyle\textsf{ROG}(H,v^{\prime})

{\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\bigcup_{f=v\in\overline{F}}\,\textsf{ROG}(H,v)\cup\{\iota\},&v^{\prime}=\iota\land H(\iota)=K\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\overline{F}\,\overline{M}\}\\
\emptyset,&\textit{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\textsf{DefVar}(H,t)

\displaystyle=\{x\mid x\in t\land x\in\textit{dom}(H)\}

Lemma B.15 (Well-formed Values Are Not Absent (Or Null)).

\Gamma\vdash H;E[v]\Rightarrow v\neq\varnothing\land v\neq\top

Proof.

By induction on the well-formedness of the configuration.
∎

Lemma B.16 (Fresh Variables).

{{\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash H;\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t\Rightarrow x\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)\land x\notin\textit{dom}(H)

{\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash H;\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{spawn}}}}}}}~(x)~\{t\}\Rightarrow x\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)\land x\notin\textit{dom}(H)

Explanation. When a term or expression introduces a new variable, the variable is new to
\Gamma
and
H
.

Proof.

Immediate by definition of well-formed configuration.
∎

Lemma B.17 (Store Contains Top-Level Variables).

{{\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash H;\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t\Rightarrow\textsf{FreeVars}(e)\cup\textsf{FreeLoc}(e)\subseteq\textit{dom}(H)

Explanation. Given a thread
{{T=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t
, the free variables
and locations of the expression
e
are in the domain of the store.

Proof.

Immediate from definition of well-formed configuration.
∎

Lemma B.18 (Weakened Well-formed Environment).

\Gamma\vdash H\land x\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)\Rightarrow\Gamma,x:K\vdash H

Explanation. If the store is a well-formed w.r.t. an environment, then
extending the environment cannot contradict this fact.

Proof.

Straightforward induction on the well-formedness rules.
∎

Lemma B.19 (Typing Permutation).

\Gamma\vdash t\land\Delta\textit{ is a permutation of }\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta\vdash t

Proof.

By induction on the typing derivation
\Gamma\vdash t
.
∎

Lemma B.20 (Weakening Typing Environment).

(1)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\land x\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)\land x\notin\textsf{Vars}(t)\Rightarrow\Gamma,x:K\vdash t

(2)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t\land\iota\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)\Rightarrow\Gamma,\iota:K\vdash t

(3)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash e\land x\notin\Gamma\Rightarrow\Gamma,x:K\vdash e

Proof.

By induction over the term well-formedness.
There are multiple cases to handle.
We show the case when
{{t=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~y~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
which is the more interesting one.
Case
{{t=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~y~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
. By the initial assumptions:

(4)

{{\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~y~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}

(5)

\displaystyle x\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)

(6)

\displaystyle x\notin\textsf{Vars}(t)

Need to show

{{\Gamma,x:K\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~y~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}

We prove each of the components of rule
WF-Let
.

(1)

By the initial assumption
{{\Gamma\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~y~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
(
4
) and
WF-Let
,
y\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)
.

(2)

By
1
and
6
, we can conclude that
y\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma,x:K)
.

(3)

For any
e
, we must prove that
\Gamma,x:K\vdash e
is well-formed.
We apply the induction hypothesis (
Eq.

3
), and conclude that
\Gamma,x:K\vdash e
is
well-formed

(4)

We need to show that
\Gamma,x:K,y:K^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}
. By the initial
hypothesis
{{\Gamma\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~y~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
(
4
). As part of the assumptions

\Gamma,y:K^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}
holds. By the induction hypothesis
Eq.

1
,
we conclude that
\Gamma,x:K,y:K^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}
.

(5)

From
3
and
4
and by typing rule
WF-Let
,
we can conclude
{{\Gamma,x:K\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~y~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
.

The proof of
Eqs.

2
and
3
follow the same proof strategy.
∎

Lemma B.21 (Weakening).

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash H;t\land\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\land(\textit{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})\cap\textit{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\textsf{Vars}(t)=\epsilon)\land H^{\prime}\supseteq H

\displaystyle\qquad\land\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime};t

Proof.

By induction on the shape of
t
. The most interesting case is
{{t=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}

and the others follow the same proof technique. We need to prove:

{{\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash H;\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}\land\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\cup\Gamma^{\prime}\land\Gamma^{\prime}\supseteq\Gamma\land H^{\prime}\supseteq H

{{\displaystyle\qquad\land\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\cap\Gamma\cap\textsf{Vars}(\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime})=\epsilon\land\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime}

{{\displaystyle\qquad\Rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime};\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}

We prove that if all components are well-formed, then by rule
WF-Term

{{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime};\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
.

(1)

From the assumptions,
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime}
.

(2)

We want to prove that
{{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
.
From the initial assumptions,
{{\Gamma\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
and

x\notin\textit{dom}(\Gamma)
and
x\notin\textsf{Vars}(t)
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\supseteq\Gamma
.
By multiple applications of
Lemma

B.20
, we conclude

{{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
.

(3)

From
1
and
2
, and by application
of
WF-Term
, conclude
{{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash H^{\prime};\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~e~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}
.

∎

Lemma B.22 (Weakening Maintains Thread Locality).

\displaystyle\overline{T}\equiv t\ \overline{T^{\prime}}\land\textsf{Local}(H,\overline{T})\land\iota\in\textsf{ROG}(H,t)

\displaystyle\quad\land\textsf{isLocal}(H,\iota)\land x\notin\textit{dom}(H)\Rightarrow\textsf{Local}((H,x\mapsto\iota),t\ \overline{T^{\prime}})

Explanation. Extending the heap with new variables does not affect thread-local
objects.

Proof.

Trivially satisfied by induction on the definition of
\textsf{Local}(H,\overline{T})
.
∎

Lemma B.23 (Binding Isolate Locations Maintain Isolatedness).

\displaystyle\overline{T}\equiv t\ \overline{T^{\prime}}\land\textsf{Isolated}(H,\overline{T})\land\iota\in t\land\textsf{isIso}(H,\iota)\Rightarrow\textsf{Isolated}((H,x\mapsto\iota),\overline{t\ T^{\prime}})

Explanation. Extending the heap with a stack variable over an isolated location
maintains isolation.

Proof.

Trivially satisfied by induction on the definition of
\textsf{Isolated}(H,\overline{T})
.
∎

Lemma B.24 (Thread-Local Term Substitution Maintains Locatity).

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\subseteq t

\displaystyle\land\textsf{Local}(H,t\ \overline{T})\land\iota\in\textsf{ROG}(H,t)\land\textsf{isLocal}(H,\iota)

\displaystyle\land x\notin\textit{dom}(H)\land\textsf{FreeVar}(t^{\prime})\subseteq\textsf{FreeVar}(t)\cup\{x\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\textsf{Local}((H,x\mapsto\iota),t^{\prime}\ \overline{T})

Explanation. If an object
\iota
is thread-local to term
t
, there is a subterm

t^{\prime}
of
t
, and we extend the heap to map a stack variable to
\iota
, then
replacement of
t
by
t^{\prime}
maintains thread-locality under the modified heap.

Proof.

By induction on the shape of
t^{\prime}
.
t^{\prime}
can only use free variables
available in
t
and the other threads cannot point to the introduced variable

x
as
x\notin\textit{dom}(H)
. Hence, thread-locality is maintained for object in
\iota
.
∎

Lemma B.25 (Deterministic Evaluation).

If
H;T\ \overline{T}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T}
and
H;T\ \overline{T}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime\prime};\overline{T^{\prime\prime}}\ \overline{T}
, then
\overline{T^{\prime}}\equiv\overline{T^{\prime\prime}}

Proof.

By induction on a derivation of
H;T\ \overline{T}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T}
.
∎

Lemma B.26 (Capability Stripping Preserves Terms and Expressions).

{(t^{i})^{e}=t^{i}[K/\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{unsafe}}}}}}}]

Explanation: A capability stripped term is equivalent to a replacement
of its capabilities
K
by
unsafe
, i.e., preserves the terms and expressions.

Proof.

By induction on the term structure.
∎

See
5.3

Proof.

By induction on the thread structure. Assume we are deadling with thread local
objects. The only way to create a thread local object with a reference to
a thread local object with different owner is by rule

R-FieldUpdate
. But this rule does not satisfy the premises when the owner of the
target and source objects have different owners. Instead, rule
E-BadFieldAssign
applies
and throws a permission error.
∎

Lemma B.27 (Unsafe Programs Do Not Raise Permission Errors).

If

\Gamma^{e}\vdash(H;\overline{T})^{e}

and

(H;\overline{T})^{e}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}
,
then

\textmd{Err}_{C}\not\equiv\overline{T^{\prime}}\not\equiv\textmd{Err}_{P}{}
.

Proof.

By induction on a derivation of the reduction derivation and the thread
configuration. Trivially, configurations that do not throw runtime errors
satisfy the inductive hypothesis; configurations where
T^{\prime}\equiv\textmd{Err}_{N}
or
T^{\prime}\equiv\textmd{Err}_{C}

trivially satisfy the hypothesis; from the reduction rules that throw permission errors:

E-AliasIso
,
E-IsoField
,
E-BadFieldAssign
,
E-CopyTarget
,
E-BadInstantiation
and
E-LocalField

require safe capabilities to throw a permission error but by the initial assumptions,
we have stripped the safe capabilities. Thus, these errors cannot happen.
Reduction rule
E-BadInstantiation
cannot happen because all capabilities are

unsafe
and
\textsf{OkRef}(...)
is trivially satisfied.
∎

See
5.1

Proof.

Assumptions
\Gamma\vdash H;\overline{T}
.

We start by induction on the shape of
\overline{T}
.

(1)

\overline{T}\equiv\textit{Err}
. Progress holds trivially.

(2)

\overline{T}\equiv\epsilon
. Progress holds trivially.

(3)

\overline{T}\equiv T\ \overline{T^{\prime}}
. Induction hypothesis:

IH1:

T=\textit{Err}\lor\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;T)\lor T=\epsilon\lor H;T\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime\prime}}

IH2:

\overline{T^{\prime}}=\textit{Err}\lor\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;\overline{T^{\prime}})\lor\overline{T^{\prime}}=\epsilon\lor H;\overline{T^{\prime}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime\prime}}

IH3:

\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;T\ \overline{T^{\prime}})

We continue by case analysis over
IH1
.

(a)

T\equiv\textit{Err}\ \overline{T^{\prime}}
. By the equivalence rules,

T\equiv\textit{Err}
and is a terminal configuration.

(b)

T\equiv\epsilon
, then we continue by
IH2
.

(c)

H;T\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime\prime}}
by
C-Eval
.

(d)

\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;T)
. We continue by case analysis over
IH2
:

(i)

\overline{T^{\prime}}\equiv\textit{Err}
. By the equivalence rules,
T\ \textit{Err}\equiv\textit{Err}
and is a terminal configuration.

(ii)

\overline{T^{\prime}}=\epsilon
. Then
\Gamma\vdash H;T
is a terminal configuration.

(iii)

H;\overline{T^{\prime}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime\prime}}
, the configuration steps to

H;T\ \overline{T^{\prime}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};T\ \overline{T^{\prime\prime}}
by rule
C-Eval
.

(iv)

\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;\overline{T^{\prime}})
, then we proceed by
IH3
,

\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;T\ \overline{T^{\prime}})
, and progress holds trivially.

(4)

\overline{T}\equiv t
. By the induction hypothesis either
\Gamma\vdash H;t
is a terminal configuration or it can make progress.

A1:

\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;t)\lor H;t\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}

We continue by case analysis over
A1
.

•

\textsf{Deadlock}(\Gamma\vdash H;t)
. Progress holds trivially.

•

H;t\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}
.

From the initial assumption and
WF-Configuration
:

Proof.

∎

See
5.5

Proof.

From
Theorem

5.2
(Preservation), and

WF-Configuration
, every configuration satisfies the

Local
and
Isolated
predicates. This means that at no
point will a local object be reachable from two threads, which
excludes these from taking part in data-races. By

R-FieldAssign
and
E-BadFieldAssign
, immutable objects
cannot be modified, which excludes these from taking part in
data-races. This leaves isolated objects. For a data-race to
happen on an isolated object
\iota
, the following must be
possible: at time
t_{0}
, a thread reads/writes
\iota
, at time

t_{1}>t_{0}
(later reduction step), another thread
reads/writes
\iota
, and nowhere between
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
is

\iota
moved from the first thread to the second. Because of

Isolated
, if there are more than one
reachable
13
13

13

Note that as we do not discard old stack
variables, there may be variables in the store which are
unreachable to the program.
pointers to
\iota
, all those
pointers are on the stack of a single thread. Thus, for two
threads to be able to reach the same isolated object without
moving it, the isolated object must be stored in a field
f

in an object
o
reachable from both threads (WLOG, assume
directly reachable from stack as we require unravelling paths
field-by-field). From before, we know
o
cannot be local or
isolated. Immutable objects cannot contain isolated objects
(
R-New
and
OkRef
). Thus, this is only possible when

o
is unsafe. Thus, we conclude that Dalarna is data-race
free modulo unsafe objects.
∎

See
5.6

Proof.

(1)

\Gamma\vdash H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\Rightarrow\Gamma^{e}\vdash(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
.

This is trivially satisfied from the definition of capability stripping (
Definition

B.11
).
Any configuration with safe capabilities can replace the safe capabilities by
unsafe
and
will still be well-formed. ∎

By induction on a derivation of

and by case analysis of
t
. Reminder: assumption
\overline{T_{1}}=\textmd{Err}_{A}\lor\textmd{Err}_{N}

(H;t\ \overline{T})^{e}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime\prime};\textit{Err}

We proceed by induction on a derivation

.

By all terms that use the derivation

,

,

,

, and

, a missing field
or method in the unsafe configuration,
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime\prime};\overline{T_{1}}\ \overline{T_{0}}
reduces using the same reduction rule
producing the same error.

•

By all terms that use the derivation

,

E-Consume
,
E-AbsentTarget
,

,

,
E-AbsentCopyTarget
, a missing target in the
unsafe configuration,
(H;t\ \overline{T})^{e}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime\prime};\textit{Err}

reduces using the same reduction rule producing the same error.

∎

(3)

(a)

If
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}\rightsquigarrow(H^{\prime};\textit{Err})^{e}

then,
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\textit{Err}

By induction on a derivation of
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
.
Need to show:

H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\textit{Err}

(i)

E-NoSuchField
,
E-NoSuchMethod
,
E-NoSuchFieldAssign
,
E-SendBadTargetOrArgument
, and
E-RecvBadTarget
. Any configuration
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
that reduces to
(H^{\prime};\textit{Err})^{e}
.
By
Lemma

B.25
with
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}
deterministically takes a single reduction step.
The fact that
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}
may have safe capabilities does not stop normal errors to occur.

(ii)

E-AbsentVar
,
E-Consume
,
E-AbsentTarget
,

E-AbsentTargetAccess
,
E-AbsentFieldAssign
,

E-AbsentCopyTarget
. From the induction hypothesis and the rules of the case,
regardless of safe capabilities, the configuration will throw a
\textmd{Err}_{A}
.

∎

(b)

If
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}\rightsquigarrow(H^{\prime};\overline{T}\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
and
\overline{T}\neq\textit{Err}
then,
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T_{0}}
and

\overline{T^{\prime}}=\textmd{Err}_{P}\lor\textmd{Err}_{C}\lor\overline{T}
.

By induction on a derivation of
(H;t\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
.
Need to show:

H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T_{0}}\land\overline{T^{\prime}}=\textmd{Err}_{P}\lor\textmd{Err}_{C}\lor\overline{T}

(i)

R-Let
. If
{{(H;\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~v~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}\rightsquigarrow(H,x\mapsto v;t^{\prime}\ \overline{T_{0}})^{e}
, then
H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H^{\prime};\overline{T^{\prime}}\ \overline{T_{0}}
and
\overline{T^{\prime}}=\textmd{Err}_{P}\lor\textmd{Err}_{C}\lor\overline{T}
.
x\notin\textit{dom}(H)
by assumption
\Gamma\vdash H;t\ \overline{T_{0}}
, and by application of
R-Let

{{H;\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{let}}}}}}}~x~=~v~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{in}}}}}}}~t^{\prime}\ \overline{T_{0}}\rightsquigarrow H,x\mapsto v;t^{\prime}\ \overline{T_{0}}
as required.

(ii)

R-Var

under assumption
\neg\textsf{isIso}(H,\iota)
. We proceed by cases analysis over the capability of
\iota
(omit the case were
H(x)=\top
, as that would have thrown an error in the hypothesis):

•

{{H(\iota)=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{iso}}}}}}}\,\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\tt{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\tt{obj}}}}}}}\,\{\_\,\_\}

∎

Theorem B.28 (Multistep Dynamic Gradual Guarantee).

•

A program
P
reduces to a terminal configuration
C
and generates a trace
\mathcal{T}
from

\epsilon;P\rightsquigarrow^{*}H;C
.

C
may or may not be an error configuration.
Let
P^{\prime}
be
P^{e}
, and let
n=|\mathcal{T}|
:

(1)

If
\textmd{Err}_{C}\neq C\neq\textmd{Err}_{P}
, then
\mathcal{T};\epsilon;P^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{n}\mathcal{T^{\prime}};H;C

(2)

If
C\equiv\textmd{Err}_{C}\lor\textmd{Err}_{P}
,
then
\mathcal{T};\epsilon;P^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{n}\mathcal{T^{\prime}};H^{\prime};\overline{T}
, and
\overline{T}\neq C

•

A program
P^{e}
reduces to a terminal
configuration
C
and generates a trace
\mathcal{T}
from
\epsilon;P^{e}\rightsquigarrow^{*}H^{e};C
.

C
may or may not be an error configuration.
Let
P^{\prime}
be
P
, and let
n=|\mathcal{T}|
. If
P^{\prime}
executes
\mathcal{T}
, then either:

(1)

if
C\not\equiv\textit{Err}
,
then
\mathcal{T};\epsilon;P^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{m}\mathcal{T^{\prime}};H^{\prime};C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\equiv C\lor\textmd{Err}_{P}\lor\textmd{Err}_{C}
and
m\leq n
.

(2)

if
C\equiv\textit{Err}
, then
\mathcal{T};\epsilon;P^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{n}\mathcal{T^{\prime}};H;C

The proof follows directly from
Theorem

5.6
.
∎
